Orientation also affects pickup, but this batch records distance without microphone angle, so a description should not invent that detail.     "response_phrase": "Channel count describes how many audio channels are stored; these entries list one or two. Microphone pattern describes the microphone’s relative sensitivity to sound arriving from different directions. For example, a figure-eight pattern picks up front and rear directions but does not inherently produce two audio channels. The batch pairs every listed microphone pattern with both channel counts, showing that these are separate properties. Two channels may carry stereo information, but the count alone does not reveal microphone arrangement or whether the channels contain distinct spatial information.",

    "response_phrase": "An exhibit label describes the object or display, while a descriptive recording tag characterizes an associated audio capture. They may share terms such as “glass” or “slate,” linking the recording’s subject to the exhibit context. A recording tag can also describe capture conditions or audible characteristics that the exhibit label does not address. This batch provides material descriptions, recording identifiers and archive labels, but no explicit exhibit-to-recording identifier mapping. Shared descriptive words therefore do not establish that two entries concern the same exhibit or the same recording.",

    "response_phrase": "OGG could reduce package size, but **the codec matters**: Ogg is a container. The fixture’s omission of OGG means **unknown compatibility**, not proven incompatibility. Either choice still requires the r17 visitor-player check before release, and archival originals remain WAV.\n\nNo files changed; FLAC remains the committed public format. Which OGG codec are you considering, and is smaller package size the main goal?",

Placement—including distance, angle, and orientation—affects the balance of direct sound and room reflections, as well as level and tonal balance. This batch records distances of 25–89 cm, which provide useful capture context but cannot alone establish how each recording sounds. A recording description should therefore state the microphone pattern and placement, while distinguishing those setup details from qualities actually observed in the audio.",

    "response_phrase": "A catalog identifier names an entry so readers can find, cite, and cross-reference its descriptive record. A storage filename names a particular digital asset and may convey its declared format through an extension. In this batch, `H18-0000` identifies the catalog entry, while `archive-18-0000.wav` labels its associated archive file. Keeping these roles separate allows a catalog entry to retain its identity even if an associated file is renamed or additional versions are created. An explicit mapping between identifiers and filenames helps readers connect the source description to the correct asset without relying solely on similar-looking names.",

    "request_phrase": "ion\":\"historical printing gallery\",\"recorded_year\":2011,\"source_material\":\"paper\",\"microphone_pattern\":\"figure-eight\",\"distance_cm\":29,\"sample_rate_hz\":48000,\"bit_depth\":24,\"channels\":2,\"duration_seconds\":94,\"archive_label\":\"archive-19-0069.wav\",\"description\":\"Descriptive recording of an exhibit made with paper; the catalog distinguishes source labels, placement notes, room reflections, and archive identifiers. Listening notes describe a historical capture and have no current device acceptance status.\"}\n\nExplain how channel count and microphone pattern differ. Answer in four to six sentences.\n",
    "response_phrase": "**Channel count** describes how many audio channels are stored; this batch lists one or two. One channel is mono, while two channels can carry stereo audio but do not alone prove a meaningful stereo image. **Microphone pattern** describes directional sensitivity—for example, cardioid favors the front, while figure-eight captures front and rear. A figure-eight microphone can feed a single channel, so its two pickup directions do not imply two recorded channels. The fields therefore describe different aspects of the recording, and neither alone establishes the complete microphone arrangement or spatial quality of the captured sound.",

Listening notes describe a historical capture and have no current device acceptance status.\"}\n\nExplain how gain notes differ from measured loudness. Answer in four to six sentences.\n",
    "response_phrase": "Gain notes document amplification or attenuation applied during recording or processing, such as a relative adjustment or control setting. They do not establish the resulting loudness, because source level, microphone placement, and other processing also affect the recorded signal. Measured loudness analyzes the actual audio over a specified interval using a defined method and unit, such as integrated LUFS. Comparing loudness measurements requires consistent methods, and those measurements still do not determine what a listener hears at a particular playback volume. This batch supplies neither gain values nor measured loudness, so its descriptive notes and technical audio fields should not be treated as loudness measurements.",

    "response_phrase": "An exhibit label identifies or contextualizes the physical source, such as its stated material and collection. A descriptive recording tag characterizes a particular audio capture, rather than establishing the exhibit’s identity. The two relate through the recording’s association with its source, so a tag should be interpreted alongside the exhibit label and capture metadata. Different microphone placements and room conditions can produce different recording descriptions of the same exhibit, so a material label alone cannot predict its recorded sound. This batch supplies `source_material` and narrative `description` fields, but no separate descriptive recording-tag field.",
